the infamous Lumma Stealer family – emerging in the wake of Lumma’s takedown and the doxxing of its alleged core members.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ede8006689bbfaba0d6fa856478b3ea79\"\u003eIn this technical blog post, we detail the compelling evidence tying Remus to Lumma across multiple dimensions.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef3f0d531ccf019ab00b98a38b3c43c17\"\u003eWe also describe a previously undocumented Application-Bound Encryption bypass employed specifically by Remus and Lumma.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee0040d0e9463ac082d24fcdbfd7af133\"\u003eThe first Remus campaigns date back to February 2026, with the malware switching from Steam/Telegram dead drop resolvers to EtherHiding and employing new anti-analysis checks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIntroduction\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the security industry talks about information stealers, Lumma Stealer, without a doubt, has become the notorious icon of this landscape. Not only could it count itself among the most sophisticated, technically advanced, and widespread stealers-as-a-service in the world, but it was also described in a variety of blog posts from basically everyone in the industry, including us.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this analysis, we describe a new variant of Lumma Stealer that we call Remus, a new x64 build which we suspected might occur in the foreseeable future after the doxxing of Lumma authors from late August to October 2025. And the future is here.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRemus brings the same stealing arsenal to the table as we already know from Lumma, capable of stealing stored browser passwords, cookies, cryptocurrency, and much more. However, rather than revisiting Lumma's well-documented capabilities, we focus on the remarkable resemblance between Remus and Lumma, as well as the new techniques Remus introduces, including the use of EtherHiding to resolve C2s, replacing the traditional use of Steam and Telegram dead drop resolvers, and additional anti-analysis checks.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attribution of Remus to Lumma is many-fold and described thoroughly throughout the blog post. The main indicators, to name a few, are the use of the same string obfuscation technique, AntiVM checks, direct syscall/sysenter handling, indirect control flow obfuscation, and, most importantly, an almost identical approach to bypassing AppBound Encryption, which we’ve only seen used explicitly by Lumma to date.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith that said, we can still see active Lumma campaigns all around the world, which makes Remus not a replacement, but a continuous evolution.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDevelopment timeline\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore diving into the technical details, it is worth noting that during our hunting efforts, we came across several test samples, which even carry a \u003ccode\u003etestbuild\u003c/code\u003e label embedded directly in the binaries (see Figure 1). We are internally referencing these builds as Tenzor (based on encrypted strings). This may suggest that some of Lumma’s authors split off, or that Lumma decided to rebrand under a new name in the midst of the doxxing fallout. Many of the techniques and design patterns we encountered in Remus closely mirrored those we had already seen in Lumma Stealer. The main difference is that Lumma was 32-bit while Remus is 64-bit, which naturally introduced some variations. Yet, when we looked past the 32-bit versus 64-bit differences and stripped away the obfuscation layers, what remained was strikingly familiar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the following subsections, we highlight the most compelling evidence of a direct connection between Remus and Lumma. Note that this is not an exhaustive list. The overlaps are far more numerous, but we selected just a few that we believe speak for themselves.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eForgotten dead drop resolver and string obfuscation\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor a malware researcher, strings are among the most valuable resources during reverse engineering, and in the case of attributing Remus to Lumma, they proved no different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma use a virtually identical mechanism for string obfuscation. The encrypted string is first assembled on the stack using a series of various forms of the \u003ccode\u003emov\u003c/code\u003e instruction, followed by a decryption loop that transforms the data byte by byte. The transformation is often further obscured by MBA (Mixed Boolean-Arithmetic) obfuscation and is either inlined or placed in a standalone function. In any case, each string is protected using a unique transformation, making it very difficult to build a universal static decryptor. As a result, emulating the decryption loops is likely the most effective approach for dealing with encrypted strings in both Remus and Lumma.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=gXkouPhN 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=VYItjdSJ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":760},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=RSIG-gtA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Sx7JfLS1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":578},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Kc1NFRAj 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=BYluRXHH 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":448}],"caption":"Figure 4: Decryption of the string “Processes.txt” in Remus (left) and Lumma (right). ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_04_0.jpg.webp?itok=Kc1NFRAj","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eNotably, the decryption loops are often preceded by several \u003ccode\u003enop\u003c/code\u003e instructions (Lumma) or a single \u003ccode\u003enop\u003c/code\u003e instruction encoded using multiple bytes (Remus), likely inserted as padding during a custom compilation pass. We are aware that the compilers themselves can emit \u003ccode\u003enop\u003c/code\u003e paddings. Still, in both Remus and Lumma, these sequences go beyond the typical, making it yet another indicator linking the two together.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter decrypting all strings in both Remus and Lumma and comparing them side by side, we found roughly 100 completely identical strings. Most of these, however, could be attributed to virtually any information stealer. The more telling discovery came when we looked at the strings that were not identical – most of them turned out to be semantically the same, just slightly refactored. This alone would already be a strong indicator.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHowever, what ultimately confirmed our theory was shifting the string comparison from Lumma vs. Remus to Lumma vs. Tenzor, as Tenzor effectively acts as a bridge between the other two – many of the strings we had previously matched only semantically between Remus and Lumma were still present in Tenzor in their original, unmodified form, directly matching Lumma’s. However, unlike most techniques relying on injection, Lumma as well as Remus do not use this shellcode to call \u003ccode\u003eIElevator::Decrypt\u003c/code\u003e with the key obtained from the \u003ccode\u003eLocal State\u003c/code\u003e file (the \u003ccode\u003e[“os_crypt”][“app_bound_encrypted_key”]\u003c/code\u003e JSON field). Instead, it locates the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly in the browser’s process memory, where it is stored in an encrypted form protected by \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. It then calls the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e with the same flag from within the browser’s context to decrypt it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt is important to note that this in-memory encrypted form of the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is protected with a \u003cstrong\u003edifferent key\u003c/strong\u003e than the one used to protect the key on disk – it is a separate protection layer that Chromium browsers use to safeguard the key while it resides in memory at runtime. However, since the key is protected with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag, decryption can only be performed by the same process that encrypted it, which is precisely why injection into the browser process is necessary in this specific bypass. For the curious, we discussed this topic in more detail in our recent\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eVoidStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom this point on, we will walk through the ABE bypass as implemented in Remus, noting any differences from Lumma’s implementation where applicable. Where no differences are explicitly mentioned, the two implementations work either identically or very similarly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo how does Remus find the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e? It begins by walking the browser’s PEB module list, looking for two modules in a single pass: \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e and the browser DLL (e.g., \u003ccode\u003echrome.dll\u003c/code\u003e).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen \u003ccode\u003edpapi.dll\u003c/code\u003e is found, Remus manually parses its PE export directory, reading the DOS header, PE header, and export table through repeated \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e syscalls. For each exported function name, it computes a seeded CRC32 hash and compares it against the pre-computed hash\u0026nbsp;corresponding to\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e. Essentially, a typical API-hashing, but in a remote process. Once a match is found, it\u0026nbsp;resolves the function’s RVA to obtain its absolute address in the browser’s address space.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the browser DLL is found during the same pass, Remus decrypts a hex pattern \u003ccode\u003e488d058bcc8d02488901488b024889415b488d41\u003c/code\u003e, which was encrypted by the same string obfuscation technique described earlier and stores the value \u003ccode\u003e0xEFF87\u003c/code\u003e (which corresponds to \u003ccode\u003e11101111111110000111\u003c/code\u003e in binary) into a variable we labeled as \u003ccode\u003ewildcard_mask\u003c/code\u003e (see\u0026nbsp;Figure\u0026nbsp;8).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_08_0.jpg.webp?itok=RG69h1KY While their implementations differ in some details, mostly as a natural consequence of the 32-bit vs 64-bit architecture, the overall design is strikingly similar.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne of the first things both do upon execution is that they enumerate all \u003ccode\u003eNt\u003c/code\u003e-prefixed exports from \u003ccode\u003entdll.dll\u003c/code\u003e and build a lookup table mapping their name hashes to the corresponding Syscall Service Numbers (SSNs). The core logic is the same: arrange the syscall/sysenter arguments and invoke the kernel. Remus does so directly via the\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003esyscall\u003c/code\u003e\u0026nbsp;instruction. Lumma, on the other hand, resolves a dispatcher address during the hash-to-SSN initialization through a fallback chain: it first attempts to resolve the \u003ccode\u003eWow64Transition\u003c/code\u003e export from \u003ccode\u003entdll.dll\u003c/code\u003e using API-hashing, then tries the \u003ccode\u003eTEB-\u0026gt;WOW32Reserved\u003c/code\u003e, and finally, if that value is \u003ccode\u003eNULL\u003c/code\u003e, falls back to a hardcoded \u003ccode\u003esysenter\u003c/code\u003estub. Each of these fallback mechanisms ultimately achieves the same outcome – a transition from user-mode 32-bit code into the kernel.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=CDx3nGug 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=j-DE0mfN 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":732},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=xEFqXbuq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=lEQ8KH3d 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":557},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=wciR39kD 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=jKGeSZqy 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":432}],"caption":"Figure 22: Remus’s syscall dispatcher. Both Remus and Lumma begin executing their malicious logic directly from the PE entry point (the\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003estart\u003c/code\u003e\u0026nbsp;function), with no runtime initialization whatsoever. In a typical C/C++ binary, the entry point calls the CRT startup routine, which sets up the heap, initializes global variables, registers exception handlers, processes the command line, and invokes static constructors before eventually calling\u0026nbsp;\u003ccode\u003emain\u003c/code\u003e. However, neither Remus nor Lumma follows this pattern. Instead, both jump directly from the entry point into their core logic: resolving modules and APIs, performing anti-analysis checks, initializing C2 communication, and exfiltrating data, ending with a direct call to \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess(0)\u003c/code\u003e. This suggests both were compiled without linking to the standard C runtime, likely a deliberate choice to minimize the binary size and reduce unnecessary dependencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe structural similarities extend further. Both binaries share the same section layout, with each section serving an identical semantic role. Even the C2 configuration follows the same pattern: in both cases, the encrypted blob, key, and nonce are statically embedded in the \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section and decrypted at runtime using ChaCha20.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo summarize, we strongly believe the degree of similarity between the two codebases is too deliberate to be a coincidence, pointing to a single, shared origin.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eIndirect control flow obfuscation\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFinally, both Remus and Lumma make use of control flow obfuscation by replacing direct jumps with indirect ones, where the target is read from an offset stored in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section. As shown in Figure 28, this technique takes several forms. In its simplest case, the target address is resolved by a single pointer dereference (highlighted in yellow). In more complex cases, it extends to a full jump table, where a computed index selects among several target addresses stored consecutively in the \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e section (highlighted in orange). Lastly, in some cases, the target address is first loaded into a register (highlighted in blue) and then jumped to via a register-based indirect jump (highlighted in pink).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=yw52wiVm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=IzpQtf-7 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":526},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=p9HiY3wT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=SsanKuYo 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":400},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=nkn_NNA0 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":310}],"caption":"Figure 28: Randomly selected disassembly code from Remus (left) and Lumma (right) highlighting the different forms of the discussed indirect control flow obfuscation.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_28.jpg.webp?itok=Vkmug0fX","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eAlthough functionally equivalent to normal jumps, the indirection\u0026nbsp;complicates the control flow analysis in two ways. First,\u0026nbsp;some of the indirect jump targets cannot be statically determined (for example, when the register holding the target address can be set in multiple different preceding basic blocks, each potentially loading a different target), leaving the control flow graph incomplete as the connections between basic blocks are lost. Second, some of these orphaned blocks end up being misidentified as standalone functions, further disrupting function boundary detection and leading to a broken, incomplete decompiler output. Notably, Figure 28 also provides another glimpse of the \u003ccode\u003enop\u003c/code\u003e paddings discussed earlier.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_29.jpg.webp?itok=874YSvKm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_29.jpg.webp?itok=ETM1BTLo 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":356},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_29.jpg.webp?itok=nwAsEhDK 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_29.jpg.webp?itok=VnKyOM5n 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":271},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_29.jpg.webp?itok=WtBHGOr9 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_29.jpg.webp?itok=cguUQW23 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":210}],"caption":"Figure 29: A portion of the .data section in Remus (left) and Lumma (right) showing the consecutively stored target addresses referenced by the indirect jumps and jump tables.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_29.jpg.webp?itok=WtBHGOr9","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eBoth binaries contain hundreds of these indirect dispatch points, and once again, if we set aside the minor differences arising from the 32-bit vs. 64-bit differences, the obfuscation is virtually identical. Although removing the obfuscation entirely requires more effort, the IDA’s decompiler output can be significantly improved by marking the referenced offsets as constants. For those interested, we covered this trick in our \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/defeating-aurastealer-obfuscation\"\u003eAuraStealer blog post\u003c/a\u003e, along with other approaches for tackling control flow obfuscation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat’s new in Remus\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHaving covered the key similarities between Remus and Lumma, let’s now focus on what is new in Remus. The core architecture is clearly inherited from Lumma. Still, several changes go beyond a straightforward 32-bit-to-64-bit port. These range from refactored strings and more thorough device fingerprinting to a different exfiltration archive format, a switch from FNV-1a to CRC32 for API hashing, and an overhauled C2 communication that has been restructured to deliver dynamic configurations incrementally rather than in a single large blob. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most significant changes, however, are the replacement of traditional Steam/Telegram dead drop resolvers with blockchain-based C2 resolution using EtherHiding, a persistence technique allowing attackers to store malware-related data (such as domains, payloads, encryption keys, among others) on blockchain as a smart contract, and the introduction of additional anti-analysis checks.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDead drop resolvers via EtherHiding\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth Remus and Lumma employ dead drop resolvers, a mechanism in which the malware does not contact its C2 server directly but instead retrieves the C2 address at runtime from an intermediary hosted on a legitimate platform. This makes the infrastructure significantly more resilient, as if a C2 domain is taken down, the dead drop can be adjusted to point to a new server without the need to distribute an updated binary. Moreover, the dead drop URLs typically reside on well-known, high-reputation services, making them more difficult for security vendors to block without collateral damage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhere the two differ is in the choice of platform. Lumma relies on Steam profiles and Telegram channels, typically encoding C2 URLs using ROT-15, while Remus goes a step further, replacing these with Ethereum smart contracts. At runtime, it sends an \u003ccode\u003eeth_call\u003c/code\u003e JSON-RPC request to a hardcoded contract address via a public RPC endpoint and extracts the C2 URL from the hex-encoded response. Because blockchain data is decentralized and immutable, there is no platform operator to report abuse to, making the dead-drop effectively immune to takedowns.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eJust like Lumma’s Steam profile URL, Remus stores the smart contract address separately from the static C2 configuration, as a stack-encrypted string. Notably, after the JSON response is retrieved, the C2 address is, at least for now, merely hex-encoded in the \u003ccode\u003eresult\u003c/code\u003e field (no other obfuscation, such as ROT-15 in case of Lumma, is applied).\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=aykiX20l 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=pL76kfRZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":670},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=YSc7ZWVs 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=Y3V3btE1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":509},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=FFY3enyR 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_30.jpg.webp?itok=mSV0E7oS 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":395}],"caption":"Figure 30: Remus resolving a C2 using EtherHiding. If either check fails, the malware silently terminates via \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess(0)\u003c/code\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first check targets sandbox and analysis tool DLLs. Remus walks the PEB's \u003ccode\u003eInLoadOrderModuleList\u003c/code\u003e, hashes the name of every loaded module using a CRC32 variant with a custom initialization constant, and compares the result against 11 pre-computed hashes stored in its \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section, each corresponding to a module commonly injected by analysis environments, including Avast sandbox (\u003ccode\u003esnxhk.dll\u003c/code\u003e), Sandboxie (\u003ccode\u003esbiedll.dll\u003c/code\u003e), Comodo sandbox (\u003ccode\u003ecmdvrt32.dll\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003ecmdvrt64.dll\u003c/code\u003e), and several others.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_32.jpg.webp?itok=-2oeii0Y 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_32.jpg.webp?itok=x665WFSB 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":689},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_32.jpg.webp?itok=NA-ZJnjT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_32.jpg.webp?itok=Sqab3Vuz 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":524},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_32.jpg.webp?itok=Yy9oArNe 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-04/remus_blogpost_figure_32.jpg.webp?itok=JxZ_seoA 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":407}],"caption":"Figure 32: Remus’s CRC32-hashed array of forbidden DLLs. We focused on various similarities and differences between Lumma and Remus, thoroughly describing the technical aspects of both. We also provided a timeline, referencing the very first test builds of Remus (historically called Tenzor), which correlate with the extensive doxxing of Lumma authors from late August to October 2025.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe key aspects that allowed us to attribute Remus as a new x64 version of Lumma are the use of the same string obfuscation technique, AntiVM checks, direct syscall/sysenter handling, indirect control flow obfuscation, and, most importantly, an almost identical approach to bypassing AppBound Encryption, which we’ve only seen used by Lumma to date.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eRemus, however, is not merely an x64 port of Lumma. It also introduces a couple of novel techniques in how it operates, including the use of EtherHiding instead of a traditional approach of using Steam/Telegram as dead drop resolvers, as well as need Photoshop for a side project. The subscription is expensive. You search for a free Mac download, land on a site that looks good enough, and within an hour, the app is running. A few weeks later, your Mac is hot even when you're not doing much. The fans stay loud. The battery drains fast. Activity Monitor shows a process with a name you do not recognize, chewing through CPU in the background. That \"free\" app may have cost you your machine's processing power, your saved passwords, your browser sessions or access to the files on your disk. That trade is more common than many Mac users think.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIn the first 48 hours after we began tracking one recent cracked software distribution wave, our protections blocked about 108,000 attempts to launch these applications on macOS systems.\u003c/strong\u003e This is a count of blocked launch attempts recorded by one of our shields, not unique devices. Of those detections, 58% came from Macs still supported by Apple, while 42% came from systems that no longer receive Apple's current support. The pattern is already clear: even for MacOS, cracked apps are still a practical delivery channel for cryptominers, infostealers, adware and long-term persistence.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe signature that keeps showing up\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOpen enough cracked Mac apps and you start to see recurring patterns. One of the patterns we see quite often is a string left by the TNT group:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e.TNT - why join the navy if you can be a pirate\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStill, this story will not be about them but rather the whole distribution pipeline around them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA crack may start as a modified version of a legitimate app with license checks removed. After that, it often moves through mirror sites, torrent uploads, forum reposts, Telegram channels and re-packed archives. Every handoff is another chance to add something extra to the file. That is the real problem. Users rarely download a package from a trusted source. They are downloading whatever the last person in the chain decided to upload.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy people still download cracked Mac apps\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCost is one of the obvious reasons. A subscription can feel absurd when you need an app for one class, one freelance project or one weekend task. There is another reason too: old hardware. Plenty of people still use older Macs that work well enough for everyday tasks, but no longer run the latest macOS release. Once that happens, the legitimate software market starts shrinking around them. New versions no longer support old releases; official downloads for old versions get harder to find and compatibility drops. The machine still works, but the software ecosystem has moved on.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly when cracked software starts to look practical. It promises a version that runs on old hardware, costs nothing and solves the problem immediately. What it does not say is that older Macs are often already at a disadvantage. They may miss newer security features and recent patches. If the installation also tells the user to weaken built-in protections, the system becomes much easier to abuse.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow cracked apps reach users\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCracked macOS software spreads through a loose supply chain. Some files come from dedicated download sites that look almost like real app stores. Others spread through torrent trackers, warez forums, Telegram channels, Discord communities and file-sharing links posted on SEO-driven \"download\" pages. The packaging changes, the mirrors change and the hostnames change. The model stays the same.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe important point is not which site was involved. It is that every re-upload, re-zip and re-pack creates another opportunity to insert a payload. The original crack may be clean, but the version on a mirror site may already tell a different story; the torrent may include an extra installer, the archive posted to a chat channel may have been modified again. There is usually no reliable way for a typical user to tell the difference before running it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=VBfED1MK 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=XxWeLl0- 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":908,"height":854},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=Vb6lPM6t 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=KcYWsV5C 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":837},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=_clBtlLq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/distribution-chain-diagram.png.webp?itok=fi2aKjgx 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":649}],"caption":"Every step between the original crack and the final download is an opportunity to add malware or unwanted software. MacOS includes several protections that are meant to stop exactly this kind of software from running freely. Gatekeeper helps block apps from unidentified developers. Notarization checks software distributed outside the App Store for known malicious content. System Integrity Protection, or SIP, helps prevent changes to critical parts of the system. Permission controls limit what apps can read and modify. And cracked software install guides often tell users to work around those protections step by step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTypical instructions often include:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eae2da4be03f3a2b670f4a813338399f9\"\u003eDisable Gatekeeper so unsigned apps can run.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb606b7c024052b2cb4a67f27b385f2ce\"\u003eBoot into Recovery Mode and disable SIP.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef4a9d11e409c539f6414805c6023c5d8\"\u003eLaunch the crack tool with administrator privileges.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e10349530c6710071bdfe76740183e59c\"\u003eGrant Full Disk Access when prompted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/terminal-screenshot.jpg.webp?itok=28oaj3UT ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/terminal-screenshot.jpg.webp?itok=h_ixIKit","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThis matters because an attacker does not need to discover an advanced exploit if the user has already done the hard part for them. If an unsigned binary gets root privileges and Full Disk Access on a system with weakened protections, it has significantly more room to do real damage. On older Macs, the risk is even worse. When the OS is already behind on security updates, turning off the protections that remain can leave the system exposed for months.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat actually gets bundled with these downloads\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eUnfortunately, these payloads are a bit like a box of chocolates; you never know what you're gonna get. We tried to go through stats to identify some main types of these unwanted payloads.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed1f60a74a2f7183221b82c6518f4a758\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCryptominers\u003c/strong\u003e, such as XMRig, aim to use computer resources to mine cryptocurrency. Thus, besides the original app, the Mac will also do a second job in the background, usually resulting in high CPU usage, loud fans and significantly reduced battery life.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee96f909440c5a529a92515c4c36aebfc\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfoStealers\u003c/strong\u003e, on the other hand, are trying to stay under the radar, collect various data on the device and send it to the control server. Usually, they are after saved passwords, cookies, session tokens, wallet data or documents as these can be monetized quickly. Remember that full disk access in step 4? That one made it a whole lot easier.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecff0fdd0b1b2cd2b26f1850f4425166b\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBackdoors\u003c/strong\u003e are usually trying to stay low and provide persistent access to the computer, be it to deploy further malware or use the computer as a staging point for future actions (e.g., email campaign). On macOS, this usually means LaunchAgents or Launch Daemons that run automatically after rebooting.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efc64564b7278b592130a283c2184891b\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAdware and Browser Hijackers\u003c/strong\u003e are less dramatic than previous categories but still annoying, nonetheless. They may redirect your search, inject ads, change browser settings and commit affiliate fraud (e.g., injecting their affiliate links) - as the monetization relies on abuse of (semi-)legitimate internet infrastructure; these are the easiest ways to monetize. To be clear, the point is not that every cracked app is booby-trapped with these payloads. Unfortunately, obtaining cracked software may require you to trust an anonymous supply chain with system-level access to your systems, with limited options to verify what you got.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Mac myth still helps to keep this pipeline alive\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA lot of people still carry some version of the old belief that Macs do not really get malware. That idea was always overstated, as this usually relied on Macs being a smaller market for malware authors than the Windows ecosystem. Today, with many threats going multi-platform, this idea is actively dangerous.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile macOS has its own built-in protections that manage to provide some level of protection, they still rely on the user installing the software from trusted sources and crucially (but unsurprisingly), the user must not turn them off to have any effect. The moment a user starts disabling Gatekeeper, turning off SIP and granting broad permissions to unsigned binaries, the system is left exposed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis combination makes cracked software on macOS quite an attractive delivery method. Leading the user to disable their AV is one of the oldest tricks in the book, but this one takes it a step further by implicitly banking on a still-popular myth to avoid raising suspicion.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIs there a way back?\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e36f761d5067b1f3b97fcb41250f533d3\"\u003eRe-enable SIP in Recovery Mode:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecsrutil enable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e13ce9ed1846fa4c90cd5595cc3c40d05\"\u003eThen re-enable Gatekeeper in Terminal:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003esudo spctl --master-enable\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3f587d06dd59561a968a5024f26dcf20\"\u003eCheck common persistence locations. Unfamiliar .plist files or oddly named helper items are worth investigating.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e~/Library/LaunchAgents/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e/Library/LaunchAgents/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e/Library/LaunchDaemons/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003cbr\u003e\u003ccode\u003e~/Library/Application Support/\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e844d0ecf4d5c86132be363b690f9a29c\"\u003eRun a full system scan using e.g., Avast for Mac or Norton Security for Mac. Running it on the \u003cem\u003eDownloads\u003c/em\u003e folder might not be enough.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ead808099598a77195a50d27149505dc1\"\u003eConsider changing important passwords. Start with email, banking, work accounts, cloud storage, and any password manager. This is especially crucial if the full system scan identified malware.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb0715b9167c2c31908d76fb610c0f80e\"\u003eWatch for follow-on activity. Look for login alerts, suspicious financial activity and unusual browser behavior that continue after removing the cracked app. Consider a clean install if the system was compromised for an extended period. The safest approach is a full macOS reinstall from Recovery Mode. Back up personal files (documents, photos — not applications), wipe the drive, reinstall macOS and restore only verified clean data.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe real price of \"free\"\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCracked software might sound attractive because it solves an immediate problem. It saves money. It keeps an old Mac useful for a little longer. It avoids a subscription that people don't want to pay. But the actual deal may look different once you include the hidden terms. Even when the original crack is benign, things may get tangled once you consider distribution, potentially including other middleman in the transaction, with each possibly vying for their share.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the goal is simply to get work done, the safer alternatives are boring but real: official older installers when vendors provide them, open-source alternatives, lower-cost one-time-purchase tools, student licensing or used hardware that can still run a supported macOS release.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOur role: Protection, not judgment\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs a company behind reputable antivirus solutions such as Avast, AVG and Norton, we want to be clear: we don't judge users by the software choices they make. We understand that people might turn to cracked software to solve their problems, be it tight budgets, old hardware that still works or needing an app for a short-term project. We get it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur job is not to be the software police.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur job is to protect you from threats that hide in the places where you might be looking for solutions.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen our detection systems flag a cracked app, it is not because we disapprove of your choice to use it. It is because we found something genuinely malicious bundled with it - a cryptominer, an infostealer, a backdoor or other malware that puts your system and data at risk.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf we block a cracked app, you can be confident that we detected something nasty lurking inside. Our goal is to let you make informed decisions about the software you install, with full knowledge of what risks come with it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFinal thought\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe quote that TNT and other groups stamp on cracked software, \"Why join the Navy if you can be a pirate?\", was about thinking differently. About building something new instead of following the established path. About creativity and independence. Using it to brand cracked software misses the point entirely. The real pirates in this story are usually not the users trying to save money or keep an old Mac useful for a little longer. They are the actors further up the chain: repackagers, mirror operators, uploaders and malware distributors who turned cracked software into a monetization engine.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is what makes this ecosystem work so well. The person downloading the app thinks they are taking a shortcut. They might be stepping into a supply chain built to extract value from them, whether that means mining cryptocurrency in the background, stealing credentials, hijacking browser traffic or keeping a foothold on the machine for later.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eStill, the end decision is not ours to make.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you make your decision, step with care.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs you might eventually realize that you did not join the Navy. You did not become a pirate. You just ended up as
